## Page 1


REDUCTIONS OF CRYSTALLINE REPRESENTATIONS
OF FRACTIONAL SLOPE < p −1
SHALINI BHATTACHARYA, EKNATH GHATE AND RAVITHEJA VANGALA
Abstract. Let p be an odd prime and let Vk,ap be the two-dimensional crystalline representation of
the Galois group of Qp of weight k ≥2 and parameter ap ∈¯Qp. We study the semi-simplification ¯Vk,ap
of the mod p reduction of Vk,ap when the slope (valuation of ap) is a positive fraction < p −1 using
the mod p local Langlands correspondence. We describe the exact shape of ¯Vk,ap for all such slopes
and all (sufficiently large, depending on the slope) weights k, as long as certain Jordan-H¨older factors
of dimension p −1 do not intervene in the computation (when k is odd), though we also provide some
criteria which further determine the shape of ¯Vk,ap in some of these exceptional cases. To keep this
paper a reasonable length, we assume that for certain bad congruence classes of k mod p, the slope
is less than the representative - taken in the range [1, p −1] - of the congruence class of k −2 mod
(p −1), which is generically the case if the slope is small. Finally, a folklore conjecture predicts that
the reduction ¯Vk,ap is irreducible for fractional slopes if k is even. We deduce this conjecture for all
fractional slopes < p −2 and all (sufficiently large, even) weights k under the aforementioned slope
assumption.

Introduction
Let p be an odd prime. In this article, we compute the mod p reductions of certain two-dimensional
crystalline representations of the Galois group GQp of Qp.
Let E be a finite extension field of Qp. There is an equivalence of categories between n-dimensional
crystalline representations of GQp defined over E and the category of n-dimensional admissible filtered φ-
modules over E induced by Fontaine’s functor Dcris. Let ap ∈E with v(ap) > 0 where v is the valuation
of ¯Qp normalized so that v(p) = 1 and let k ≥2. Let Vk,ap be the irreducible two-dimensional crystalline
representation of GQp over E with Hodge-Tate weights (0, k −1) such that Dcris(V ∗
k,ap) = Dk,ap, where
( )∗denotes contragredient and Dk,ap is the filtered φ-module defined in [Ber11, §2.3]. The local p-adic
Galois representation at p of a normalized cuspidal newform of weight k ≥2, level coprime to p, trivial
nebentypus at p and with p-th Fourier coefficient ap, is known to be Vk,ap.

Let ¯Vk,ap be the semi-simplification of the reduction of a lattice in Vk,ap modulo the maximal ideal of
the ring of integers of E. It is well-known that ¯Vk,ap is independent of the choice of lattice. It is an open
problem to determine whether ¯Vk,ap is irreducible or reducible, let alone its exact shape.
In recent decades, ¯Vk,ap has been studied extensively. The exact shape of ¯Vk,ap for k ≤2p + 1 was
determined by the work of Fontaine-Edixhoven and Breuil in [Edi92] and [Bre03b]. The shape of ¯Vk,ap
when the slope v(ap) is greater than ⌊k−2
p−1⌋was determined by Berger, Li and Zhu, in [BLZ04]. A variant
of this bound, namely ⌊k−1
p ⌋, was provided by Bergdall and Levin [BL22].
The reduction ¯Vk,ap has also been completely determined when 0 < v(ap) < 2 by the work of Buzzard,
Gee, Ganguli, Ghate, Bhattacharya, Rozensztajn and Rai in [BG09], [BG13], [GG15], [BG15], [BGR18],
[GR25] (some of these results assume p ≥5; more recently [GM] have treated the case of v(ap) = 2 and
Nagel-Pande [NP25] have partially treated the case v(ap) ∈(2, 3)). In this article, we determine the
reduction ¯Vk,ap for v(ap) < p −1 for all fractional slopes less than p −1, i.e., for
v(ap) ∈(i, i + 1)
for 0 ≤i < p −1.

However, when i < a, we show in Theorem 4.6, 4.7 that only the irreducible possibility occurs, under

the expression in (1.1) has minimal valuation. (We do not work out what happens when b = 2i + 1 and
i ≥a.)
Theorem 1.1 treats the so called good congruence classes of r mod p in that it avoids certain trickier bad
congruence classes of r mod p. A large portion of this paper is devoted to treating these bad congruence
classes. To keep this paper within reasonable limits, we assume that i < a so that b = a. This is not
a serious assumption if i is small (which was the case for many of the papers mentioned earlier in this
introduction which this paper attempts to generalize).
When a ≥2i + 1, the bad congruence classes are r ≡a −i + 1, . . . , a mod p, whereas for i < a ≤2i,
they are r ≡a −i −1, . . . , a mod p. Equivalently, these congruence classes are
r ≡a −i + n mod p
for some 1 ≤n ≤i (if a ≥2i + 1) or −1 ≤n ≤i (if i < a ≤2i). For each such value of n, we define
s = a −i + n + (i −n)p

local constancy of reductions [Ber12] and the work of [BLZ04], [BL22] one has that in many cases
¯Vk,ap ≃¯Vs+2,ap ≃¯Vs+2,0 for t and v(ap) is sufficiently large. The last reduction has been completely
determined by Breuil [Bre03b]. This shows the importance of the parameters s and t. In this paper,
we go much further and try to determine which sub-quotient in the theta filtration surjects onto ¯Θk,ap
for each value of t ≥1. This usually determines the reduction ¯Vk,ap using the mod p local Langlands
correspondence.

Part (i) follows from Theorem 5.4 and Theorem 5.5. Part (ii) follows from Theorem 5.5. Part (iii)
follows from Theorems 5.34 and 5.35.
In part (ii) (b) and (iii) (b) of Theorem 1.4, there is the possibility that a twist of the JH factor Vp−2
contributes to the reduction, and it becomes difficult to specify the reduction without more work. In the
former case, we note that, in fact, a local constancy argument guarantees that this factor appears (and
contributes reducibly) for t ≫0. In the latter case, taking n = 1 and a = 2i −1 so that t = 1, it turns
out that again a twist of Vp−2 appears and contributes both irreducibly and reducibly, e.g., when i = 2
(see Remark 5.36).
In part (iii) (e) of Theorem 1.4, it is proved that Vp−2 definitely contributes if a = 2i −2n + 1 and
t ≥n+1. Thus, ¯Vk,ap could again possibly be reducible. In particular, if n = 1, the reducibility question
arises again when a = 2i −1 and t ≥2.
We offer the following rather delicate theorem when a = 2i −1, n = 1 and t ≥1, which shows that
the reduction is generically irreducible.

It is a folklore conjecture, attributed to Breuil, Buzzard, and Emerton, that if k is even and v(ap) is
not an integer, then ¯Vk,ap is irreducible. The results above allow us to make considerable headway on
this conjecture for slopes up to p −2.
Corollary 1.7. Let p ≥3 and v(ap) ∈(i, i + 1) for 0 ≤i < p −2 and let k −2 =: r ≥4i + 4 with
r ≡a mod (p −1) with 1 ≤a ≤p −1. If k is even, then ¯Vk,ap is irreducible if either
(i) i < a, or
(ii) i ≥a, and r is in a good congruence class mod p.

SHALINI BHATTACHARYA, EKNATH GHATE AND RAVITHEJA VANGALA
similar strategy. This time, the deep JH factors of Q(i) that survive are explained by a superdiagonal
pattern, see Figure 5.5. In the intermediate region 2i −2n −1 < a < 2i, the surviving JH factors are
determined in Section 5.2 and are explained by a striking hybrid version of the previous two patterns
(with an additional line segment parallel to the anti-diagonal!). See Figure 5.3 for the case of a odd
and Figure 5.4 for the case of a even. The patterns in these figures took several years to determine and
the final results caught us by surprise. The proofs of these patterns involve the construction of certain
master functions which contain certain constants βl whose existence depends on the evaluation of certain
large determinants built out of double binomial sums. We decided to give complete details about the
computation of these determinants, partly explaining the size of this paper. Finally, in the boundary
case n = i, the JH factors of Q(i) that survive are determined in Section 5.4.
All the results in Theorems 1.1 through 1.6 now follow from the mod p local Langlands correspondence.

2.
Preliminaries
In this chapter, we recall and prove some combinatorial results, some facts about the theta filtration
and some facts about Hecke operators.
2.1.
Congruences for binomial coefficients and sums
In this section, we study congruences between binomial coefficients and sums of binomial coefficients.
These congruences will be used in the later chapters in the Hecke operator computations.

The following result describes binomial coefficients mod p.
Lemma 2.1 (Lucas’ theorem). For any prime p, let m and n be two non-negative integers with base p
expansions given by m = mkpk + mk−1pk−1 + · · · + m0 and n = nkpk + nk−1pk−1 + · · · + n0 respectively.

To this end, we first study
congruences between values of polynomials at two integers that are p-adically close.
Lemma 2.2. Let p be an odd prime. Let r, s be positive integers such that r ≡s mod pt for some t ≥1.
Let α1, . . . , αN ∈Zp and f(X) = (X −α1) · · · (X −αN) be a polynomial.

Proof. Let g(X) = f(X) −f(s). As g(s) = 0, we get g(X) = (X −s)h(X) for some h(X) ∈Zp[X].
Further, note that h(s) = g′(s), where g′ denotes the derivative of g. Thus
f(r) −f(s) = g(r) = (r −s)h(r) ≡(r −s)h(s) ≡(r −s)g′(s) mod pt+1.
(2.1)

2.2.
Combinatorial lemmas for determinants
In this article, we need to solve certain linear equations or linear congruences. We solve these us-
ing Cramer’s rule. This involves showing a matrix is invertible, which in turn involves computing its
determinant. In some cases, we need to determine the p-adic valuation of the determinant.
We begin by recalling a result of Gessel-Viennot computing the determinant of a matrix whose entries
are given by binomial coefficients.
Lemma 2.9. [GV85, p. 308 & l. -7] Let k be a positive integer and a1 < a2 < · · · < ak be a sequence of
positive integers. For every non-negative integer n, we have

2.5.
The mod p LLC
In this section, we briefly recall the statement of the mod p local Langlands Correspondence (mod
p LLC). For details, we refer the reader to [Bre03a]. Recall that G = GL2(Qp), K = GL2(Zp) and
Z = Q×

• if λ = 0, then ind(ωr+1

) 7→π(r, 0, η)
• if λ ̸= 0, then (µλ · ωr+1 ⊕µλ−1) ⊗η 7→π(r, λ, η)ss ⊕π(Jp −3 −rK, λ−1, ηωr+1)ss, where ss in
the superscript denotes the semi-simplification and Jp −3 −rK denotes the congruence class of

Let Vk,ap be the two dimensional crystalline representation of the Galois group of Qp with Hodge-
Tate weights (0, k −1) and slope v(ap) from the introduction. The completion of the locally algebraic
representation Πk,ap corresponds to Vk,ap under the p-adic local Langlands correspondence. Moreover,
by the work of Berger [Ber10] (or the functorial construction of Colmez [Col10]), there is a compatibility
between the p-adic and mod p LLC with respect to the process of mod p reduction. In particular, ¯Θk,ap
corresponds to ¯Vk,ap under the mod p LLC. This allows us to transfer the problem of computing the
reduction ¯Vk,ap on the Galois side to the computation of ¯Θk,ap on the automorphic side.
The following useful consequence of the mod p LLC allows us to write down ¯Vk,ap explicitly in the
introduction using the results of §4 and §5.
Lemma 2.21. Let k ≥2 and r := k −2. If there is a surjection indG
KZ(V (m)
r

r
) ↠¯Θk,ap for some

2
).
Proof. Under the assumption r ̸≡2m ± 1 mod (p −1), it follows from (2.12) that V (m)
r

r
does
not have a JH factor which is a twist of Vp−2. It follows from the explicit description of the mod p LLC
that one of the two JH factors of V (m)
r
/V (m+1)
r
dies in ¯Θk,ap and the other contributes irreducibly. This
yields the lemma (amazingly, the answer is independent of the JH factor that survives).
□

The importance of this module stems from the fact that if v(ap) ∈(i, i + 1) for i ≥0, then there is a
surjective map

factors of Q(i) for all 0 ≤i ≤p −1 using the results obtained in [GR22].

corollaries above give a complete treatment of the structure of ¯Θk,ap for good congruence classes of r
mod p away from such cases.

5.2.
The case 2i −2n ≤a < 2i
In this section, we prove a more complicated conjecture than the diagonal conjecture of the previous
section. We do use some results from the previous section. For instance to eliminate JH factors above
the diagonal, we use Theorem 5.10. The final results and pictures appear at the end of the section.

Remark 5.20. We now make some remarks on the proof of the above theorem.
(1) For the convenience of the reader we summarize the idea of the proof since it is a bit more
complicated than previous proofs. One starts with the function f21. To smoothen T −f21 we
introduce f1. As T +f1 doesn’t vanish, one defines f3 so that T −f3 cancels T +f1. To smoothen
the remaining terms of T −f3 one introduces f22.
(2) If p2 | d and v(a2
p) ≥2i + 1, then (T −ap)(f21 + f1) suffices to conclude. Indeed, if p2 | d, then
by (5.40), we have T +f1 vanishes. Thus we don’t need f3 and hence f22.
(3) By Lucas’ theorem,
